§ 33(1)-Improvements to Property Required by Multiple Residence Law. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 86. 

At the 1952 Session of the Legislature, the "Multiple Residence Law" was amended so as to make it apply to all multiple dwellings having three or more families residing therein in all cities of the State of less than 500,000 popula- 270

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

al tion and in all towns and villages. The owner of a threefamily house occupied by three rent-paying tenants has been served with a notice by the local authorities requiring him to do certain work in and about the building, so as to comply with the provisions of this law. The owner of the building has requested information whether the cost of such work can be the basis for increases in the maximum rents paid by the tenants. 

Any work done by a landlord in and about a building occupied by tenants so as to comply with the requirements of the "Multiple Residence Law," which became effective on July 1, 1952, would be deemed to be a major capital improvement necessary for the preservation or maintenance of the housing accommodations and resulting in an increase in the rental value of the housing accommodations within the meaning of Section 33(1) of the Regulations. Since the improvement is required by law for the protection of the tenants, the consent of the tenants is not required. 

If the landlord requests information dealing with this opinion, he should be advised that upon furnishing details of the alterations, plans and costs, he may obtain a prior opinion which will indicate the rent increases which would be allowed upon completion of the improvement. Upon receipt of such a written prior opinion and after the work has been completed, he may then file an application with the Local Rent Office for the increases contained in the prior opinion.

Generally, the increase in the maximum rents to be granted by the Local Rent Administrator will permit the landlord to recover the cost of the improvement over at period of not more than ten years and will be apportioned equitably among all units in the building, including commercial and professional units. 271

Further information and any necessary forms in accordance with this Opinion may be secured at the specific Local Rent Office having jurisdiction over the area in which the building is located. 

§ 33 (1)—Increased Services or Equipment as Consideration for Execu tion of Voluntary Leases Not a Basis for Adjustment of Maximum 

Rents Under Section 33(1) of the Regulations. Administrator's Opinion No. 92. 

The maximum rent for an apartment was $40.00 per month. On March 15, 1951 the landlord and tenant entered into a lease which complied with all of the requirements of Section 33(2) of the Regulations. As a consideration for the execution of the lease the landlord replaced the mechanical refrigerator and gave the tenant the privilege of installing and maintaining a television antenna on the roof of the landlord's premises. These terms were contained in the written lease which provided for a monthly rent of $46.00. Since the basic March 1, 1943 maximum rent was increased 15% by reason of the voluntary lease, the Equalization Adjustment provision of the Act does not result in a change of the maximum rent. The landlord requests advice as to whether he may obtain an adjustment of the maximum rent after May 1, 1953 pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations for the replacement of the mechanical refrigerator and the privilege of maintaining the television. antenna on the roof of his premises. 

The landlord may not obtain any increase in the maximum rent. When the landlord entered into the voluntary lease, the additional equipment and services were merged into the overall rent provided by the lease. The additional equipment and services have become incorporated in the rent established by the execution of the lease which resulted in the landlord obtaining a higher maximum rent. 272

(1) A tenant who pays for the electricity supplied to his apartment directly to the utility company wishes to install an air-conditioning unit which will rest on part of the window sill and will be secured from the inside. unit will be entirely within the window line with exception of about six inches which will protrude beyond the window sill, and no part of the unit will be attached to or touch the outside of the building in any way. § 33(1)-Partial Commercial Use—Adjustments of Maximum Rent. Administrator's Opinion No. 100. 

A landlord has requested advice as to whether premises which are used by a tenant for combined business and dwelling purposes, and are subject to the Regulations, may have their maximum rents increased because of such usage. 

In general, this is dependent upon the situation in existence on March 1, 1950, the date determining the maximum rent. If on and prior to that date an apartment was actually used for combination residential and professional or commercial purposes the landlord may not obtain any increase in the maximum rent since this usage is a service actually enjoyed by the tenant on that date. 

However, where on March 1, 1950, the apartment was used solely for residential purposes and the tenant subsequently commences to use the housing accommodations for mixed residential and professional or commercial purposes and does derive some income from this usage, the landlord is entitled to an adjustment pursuant to Section 33(1). 

The Local Rent Administrator should inquire into the extent of the additional usage of the apartment and of the common facilities of the building by members of the 275

public who deal with the tenant in connection with the practice of his profession or business. Where this is completely lacking, as for example in the case of a professional writer who does his writing in his home, no adjustment. should be granted. Inquiry should also be made into whether the tenant has an office elsewhere in which he practices his profession or business, and whether he advertises his home as his office or place of business. For example, if the tenant is an architect who uses his home as his office, and there is the element of income derived by him of using his home as his office an adjustment is proper. 

On the other hand, if the tenant teaches drafting and prepares his work assignments in his home, no adjustment would be proper.

The Local Rent Administrator will consider all the equities in determining the amount of the adjustment. In order to establish uniform administration it is suggested that a 10% increase would be proper where the tenant derives only part of his income from his profession or the conduct of his business in the apartment. The first type of case would be that in which a dentist who had an office elsewhere had also developed the custom of seeing an occasional patient in the evening at his home, and has therefore installed a dental chair, etc. This would be a limited usage which would warrant a 10% increase. 

On the other hand, if the only office of the dentist were in his own home, and the dentist were not in the category of a retired dentist who occasionally does a little work, it would follow that a substantial income would be derived by him from the practice of his profession, and that therefore an adjustment of 33 1/3% of the maximum rent would be equitable. 276

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 33(1)-Rewiring of an Inadequately Wired Building by Landlord as Basis for Increase of Maximum Rent Under Section 33(1)(c) of the Regulations. Administrator's Opinion No. 117. 

A landlord requests advice from a Local Rent Office as to whether the rewiring of an inadequately wired building would constitute a major capital improvement within the provisions of Section 33(1)(c) and would therefore not require the consent of the tenants in the building. He also asks the amount of the rent increases which would be granted for such rewiring.

The Administrator has consistently held that the rewiring of an inadequately wired building is a major capital improvement reasonably required for the preservation and maintenance of a building and that it is not necessary for the landlord to obtain the consent of his tenants for such an improvement and the resulting increase in rent. This position has been sustained by the courts. Initially, the 

Administrator also adopted a cost amortization basis for calculating rent adjustments, providing for a seven year recoupment of the investment.

The Commission has had over three years of operating experience with this complex problem, and after extensive and independent research, is now making changes in rewiring policies and procedures.

The Administrator will continue to adhere to his previous position that the rewiring of an inadequately wired building is a major capital improvement and that it is not necessary for the landlord to obtain the consent of his tenants therefor. This position is adopted because the tremendous and continuously accelerating increase in tenants' use of electric current is rendering rewiring necessary for the operation, preservation and maintenance of many structures. Wiring systems installed relatively recently, as well as earlier installations, have in many cases become 277

inadequate to supply needed electricity. Since inadequate current reduces voltage, appliances frequently give unsatisfactory performance and are often damaged. Far more serious, however, are the fire hazards which exist in inadequately wired buildings. These hazards increase as tenants resort to dangerous expedients in order to plug in more attachments and appliances than existing wiring can safely accommodate.

After detailed study and investigation, the Administrator has now determined that a rental value schedule should be established governing rent adjustments for rewiring as the most effective method of eliminating abuses which have seriously impaired the merit of the cost recoupment formula. Under this earlier arrangement, the costs of electric rewiring have tended to become excessive and continue to rise on a somewhat uniform basis. The limited number of electrical contractors doing this work has resulted in the absence of true competition in this branch of the industry. 

Moreover, the landlord lacks his usual incentive to keep his costs to the absolute minimum when rent increases are based upon the amount of the expenditure. Hidden finance charges and other collusive agreements resulting in exaggerated costs are possible under these circumstances. At the same time, rewiring jobs are becoming more and more extensive. Earlier applications were based mainly upon installation of heavier wiring which permitted full use of kitchen equipment and usual electrical appliances but did not provide for air conditioners. Today, more owners are filing for rewiring which would take care not only of the present electrical current needs but of future needs as well, particularly air conditioning.

All of these factors have led to a steady rise in the cost of rewiring far in excess of rises in labor and material costs. 

In 1954, for example, the average rewiring rent increase 278

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

granted was approximately $2.00 per month. By 1957 it. had risen some 75 per cent to about $3.50 per month in apartments, most of which have no air conditioners in operation.

There is ample precedent for establishment of a rental value for electrical rewiring since many other items of service and equipment have specific amounts established in the Rental Value Schedule promulgated by the Administrator. The Administrator has further studied the problem of whether the rent increase should vary depending upon the number of rooms in the apartment, and has come to the conclusion that because the benefit of rewiring of the type under discussion does not vary over-much with the size of the apartment the increase should be uniform. 

Where rewiring provides for both immediate and long term needs it is equitable for controlled tenants to pay rent increases for that portion of the cost of the installation which is of immediate benefit. This increase is directly related to a capital improvement which will do away with the fire hazard present in many under-wired buildings. 

Its amount reflects the benefits derived from better wired kitchens and apartment branch circuits relieved from overloads, thereby enabling the tenant to make better use of electrical equipment in other rooms. However, the Administrator is of the opinion that it would be inequitable to compel tenants to pay immediate rent increases for heavy wiring installed by the owner with an eye to future needs of the building. A tenant who does not have an air conditioner should not pay a rent increase based upon the cost of rewiring for air conditioners unless and until he makes use of this facility.

A full year of investigation and analysis was necessary in order to establish the fixed rental values for electrical 279

rewiring. Various sources of information were drawn upon, including records of the hundreds of applications processed by the Commission, the hearings on protest proceedings and the literature in the field. The Administrator also retained an experienced civil engineering consultant who has assisted the Administrator in analyzing the material contained in the protest files. Based on these studies, the Administrator has determined that the sum of $2.75 per month fairly represents the added rental value to an apartment which has been properly rewired to meet the normal needs of modern appliances, exclusive of air conditioning. The landlord, upon application pursuant to 

Section 33(1)(c) will become entitled to receive a rent increase in the sum of $2.75 per month for each controlled unit, provided the rewiring meets the requirements of the local electrical code.

In those buildings where the owner does a more extensive electrical job, which permits the proper utilization of air conditioning units, the landlord will become entitled to an additional rent increase of $1.00 per month for each air conditioning unit installed by the tenant. It should be noted that this $1.00 per month is the additional allowance to the landlord for the extra heavy wiring previously installed and the cost of the final connection. For this charge the landlord will be obligated to install all needed wiring within the apartment connecting the tenant's air conditioning unit with the apartment fuse panel box. It should be noted that the landlord may also be entitled to further rent adjustments based upon the tenant's installation of an air conditioning unit where it extends more than six inches beyond the window sill, or where part of the unit is attached to the outside of the building in any way. Because of different court decisions, tenants in the 

Counties of Kings, Queens, Richmond and Nassau may have 280

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

to pay this increase whenever the air conditioning unit protrudes beyond the building wall. 

In those instances where a landlord hesitates to undertake such a capital improvement under the allowances set forth in the above rental schedule, he has access to Section 33(1)(d) of the Regulations. This section provides that where a landlord secures the written consent of 75% of the tenants he may apply for rent increases; in such instances, the rental schedule herein will not apply. 

PROCEDURES 

There will no longer be any necessity for a landlord to request a prior opinion in rewiring applications. After completion of the rewiring, a landlord may file his applications for rental increases pursuant to Section 33(1)(c). 

In his application he will be required to describe briefly the type of wiring which had previously been in the building, and the type of heavier wiring now installed. This description must include a specific statement as to the capacity formerly available and the capacity available as a result of the rewiring, and proof that the rewiring system meets the minimum requirements of the local electrical code. 

The application should also show that there is at least one heavy duty appliance circuit having one or two duplex outlets or receptacles in each kitchen. In apartments which have heretofore had only one branch circuit for all existing outlets, the application should show that the existing wiring has been split so as to provide two branch circuits in addition to the new circuit and outlets installed in the kitchen. The landlord must establish this substantial improvement to the kitchen and apartment wiring before he becomes entitled to the increase of $2.75 per month set forth in this opinion. 281

Where the landlord has installed heavier wiring making it possible for the tenants to install air conditioners, the landlord will receive an additional rent increase of $1.00 per month for each air conditioning unit upon application filed pursuant to Section 33(1)(a) after the tenant has installed an air conditioning unit.

Where the tenant, with the permission of the landlord, has already installed heavier wiring for air conditioning which meets code requirements at his own expense, he will not be required to pay any extra charge even if the landlord replaces this wiring with other wiring.

The Administrator deems it proper to note that a landlord who has rewired a building so as to provide for air conditioning needs, and desires to receive rent adjustments pursuant to this Opinion, cannot unreasonably deny any tenant in the structure the right to install an air conditioning unit. The landlord may not demand, as a condition of his permission, that the tenants sign a lease increasing the maximum rent pursuant to Section 33(2).

The Administrator also deems it appropriate to state that landlords who have received prior opinion orders from the Local Rent Offices which were not protested, or which were affirmed upon protest proceedings, and who, relying upon such prior opinion orders had begun or completed their rewiring, will receive rent increases based upon such prior opinion orders. However, landlords who received prior opinion orders from Local Rent Offices which were either remanded after protest or which are still pending in the Principal Office, will receive the increases set forth herein. Landlords who, despite the pendency of protest proceedings, took the chance that the prior opinion would be sustained and proceeded with the rewiring conversion without waiting for the determination of the protest by the 282

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Administrator, will also receive increases pursuant to this 

Administrator's Opinion. 

It is also proper to note that as a condition of receiving orders increasing maximum rents the landlord must certify and establish that he is maintaining essential services. 

§ 33(1) Effect of Tax Abatement on Rent Increases Otherwise Allowable for the Installation of Central Heating and Hot Water Systems and Other Improvements. Administrator's Opinion No. 120 (For 

New York City only).

The Facts: 

A ten-family house in the City of New York has had a central heating and hot water system installed recently in contemplation of the requirements of the New York City Multiple Dwelling Code. The landlord has filed an application for rent increases pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. The landlord states that he will apply to the City of New York for tax abatement relief (See Local Law No. 688 for the Year 1956; § J41–2.4 of the New York City Administrative Code). The Local 

Rent Administrator requests advice as to the amount of rent increases he may allow by reason of the installation of the central heating and hot water system or for any other improvement to a property where the landlord receives tax abatement.

Opinion: 

The problem presented here is one which is peculiar to the City of New York. It arises from the requirement of the New York City Multiple Dwelling Code that multiple dwellings containing ten or more units have central heating and hot water systems installed by November 1, 1958 and buildings containing less than ten units have such installations by November 1, 1959. As a corollary to this law the 283

City also made provision for a tax exemption for improvements (defined in the law to be physical changes in an existing dwelling other than painting, ordinary repairs, normal replacement or maintenance items) for a twelve-year period and for further relief by way of tax abatement which will permit the recovery of 75% of the cost of the improvement over a period of nine years. This tax abatement provision applies not only to central heating and hot water but to other improvements such as the installation of private toilets or any other improvement for which the City will allow relief. However, in order to obtain this relief the landlord is required by the Administrative Code (§ J41-2.4) 

to do the following: (1) obtain a certificate from the City Planning Commission that the improvement "will not unduly interfere with projected public improvements or the clearance and rebuilding of substandard and insanitary areas"; (2) obtain from the Department of Buildings a certificate that the building is structurally sound, complies with applicable provisions of law and provides central or other appropriate and approved heating; (3) obtain a certification from the Department of Buildings of the reasonable cost of the improvement; and (4) file an application with the Tax Commission between 

February 1st and March 15th for the relief afforded by 

§ J41-2.4 of the Administrative Code. 

In a report prepared by the City Administrator in connection with the legislation referred to above, entitled "Leg islative Program To Improve Housing In New York City," 

it was stated that: "This legislation will facilitate financing for owners of cold-water buildings who will be required by the new Multiple Dwelling Code to install central heating and hot water plants. It will also help owners who desire to 284

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

rehabilitate and improve substandard multiple dwellings. 

The purpose of the improvements must be to remove fire or health hazards, or to replace inadequate or obsolete sanitary facilities."

It was further stated in the report that: "By enabling owners to amortize 75% of the cost of improvements through tax reductions, two important objectives are achieved: First, the cost of the improvements to the owner is diminished by three-quarters; second, rent increases to tenants in these properties will be substantially lower than they would otherwise be, since the net cost of the improvements to the owner is a small fraction of the full amount."

In establishing existing rental increases incident to improvements of the type affected by tax abatement, the Administrator has been mindful of the fact that many of the housing accommodations involved afford shelter to lowincome tenants. Accordingly, rental increases have been carefully scrutinized by the Administrator and set at the lowest level consistent with giving due consideration to the landlord's recoupment of capital costs and costs of operation and maintenance, if any. Since a portion of the cost of the capital improvement will be reimbursed to the landlord through tax abatement, it is clearly equitable to reduce the rental increase normally allowed for the improvement.

The Administrator, therefore, in giving effect to the legislative admonition to prevent undue "hardship and dislocation" in the administration of the Emergency Housing Rent

Control Law and bearing in mind the intent of the City administration in enacting the tax abatement legislation, has determined that the tenants should receive the benefit of two-thirds of the tax abatement allowed to the landlord 285

for the improvement. This will accomplish two things. As a result of this formula the increase in rents for low-income tenants resulting from such improvements will be less. 

Meanwhile landlords who avail themselves of tax abatement will recoup their investment over a shorter period of time because of the combination of the permitted rent increases plus tax abatement.

In a proper case the City will allow the landlord a tax abatement of 75% of the reasonable cost of the improvement amortized over a period of nine years. That means that for every $1,000.00 found to be the reasonable cost of the improvement the City will yield the landlord $750.00 in tax abatement via annual tax remissions of $83.33 each year. 

The Commission will, however, allow two-thirds of this tax abatement to be used to offset any rent increase which would otherwise be allowable for the improvement. Concretely, assume at situation were presented to the Local Rent Administrator where a building consisting of ten fiveroom apartments qualifies for tax abatement and the landlord was granted such abatement in the sum of $360.00 for each year. This translated into monthly rents for each of the ten apartments would indicate that the monthly tax abatement allocable to each apartment would be $3.00. Twothirds of this amount, or $2.00 per month, would be deducted from the amount the tenant would be required to pay in the absence of tax abatement. 

Where tax abatement has been granted by the City and the landlord is already receiving the benefits thereof the rent increase orders will reflect a rent increase which is modified by tax abatement. Where tax abatement has been granted by the City which will become effective on the following July first the rent increase orders will reflect tax abatement effective on the date when the tax abatement goes into effect. 286

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Where tax abatement has been allowed for an improvement which was the basis for a previous rent increase, the Local Rent Administrator will re-open the proceeding in order to give the tenant credit for two-thirds of the tax abatement allowed pursuant to the second unnumbered paragraph of Section 33 of the Rent and Eviction Regulations which reads as follows: "The Administrator shall have the power to revoke or modify any adjustment granted hereunder if there has been substantial change in the basis upon which such adjustment was granted." 

In computing this credit the Local Rent Administrator will base his computation upon the annual tax abatement allowed by the City. This amount will be allocated to each tenant on a monthly basis. This monthly credit will be used to reduce the rental increase previously ordered and the maximum rent will be accordingly modified effective with the date of the order unless the tax year in which tax abatement has been granted has not already commenced. In the latter case the modification will take effect on July first following the date of issuance of the order. 

Issued December 19, 1957. 

§ 33(2)-Prior Opinions. Administrator's Opinion No. 37 [Revoked]. 

§ 33 (2)-Effect of Escalator Clauses in Previous Leases. Administrator's Opinion No. 73. 

A landlord requests advice as to whether the voluntary lease increase authorized under Section 33 (2) of the new Regulations permits the landlord to collect an automatic increase in rent pursuant to the following types of escalator clauses: 

1. The tenant is in possession under a lease, the term of which has not yet expired. This lease contains the follow- 287

ing clause: "Notwithstanding the rent specified in this lease (which is the present maximum legal rent for the demised premises under existing law), tenant agrees to pay to the landlord any higher legally permissible rent or any higher maximum legal rent for the demised premises which it may become legally permissible to charge. Such increased rent shall be the rent due and payable hereunder as of the date on which it becomes legally permissible to charge said increased rent.” 

2. The tenant is in possession under a lease, the term of which has not yet expired. This lease contains the following clause: "If, by operation of law, it becomes permissible to charge a higher rent than the present maximum legal rent for the demised premises conditioned upon the landlord and tenant entering into a new agreement of lease upon such conditions as to effective date, term or otherwise as may be specified by applicable law or regulations issued thereunder, tenant agrees at the option of landlord and upon demand, to enter into such a lease at the permissible increased maximum legal rent for the demised premises and thereupon said new lease shall supersede the present lease." 

3. The tenant is in possession under a lease, the term of which has not yet expired. This lease contains a clause which reads substantially as follows: "Notwithstanding the rent specified in this lease (which is the present maximum legal rent collectible under existing law), tenant agrees to pay to the landlord when it may become legally permissible to collect same, the monthly rent of $ that being the maximum rent as established by order of The Office of The Housing Expediter. Such increased rent shall be the rent due and payable hereunder as of the date on which it becomes legally permissible to charge such increased rent." 288

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

4. The tenant is in possession as a statutory tenant, since his lease has expired and has not been renewed. A clause similar to one of the three cited above appeared in the lease under which this statutory tenant previously held possession.

There is no automatic increase in rent authorized under the Regulations. A landlord may not demand or receive more than the present maximum rent in any of these cases. 

These escalator clauses cannot be deemed voluntary written agreements within the meaning of Section 33(2). This section of the Regulations contemplates a new voluntary lease executed by landlord and tenant in accordance with the provisions of that section of the Regulations. The escalator clauses referred to above are therefore inoperative to automatically increase the maximum rent. 

§ 33(2)—Cancellation Clauses in Voluntary Leases. Administrator's Opinion No. 74. 

A landlord requests advice as to whether certain types of clauses giving the landlord the right to cancel, may be inserted in voluntary leases executed under Section 33(2) of the Regulations.

The first proposed clause reads as follows: 

"In the event that during the term hereof the rent herein fixed shall, for reasons other than the fault of the landlord, be in excess of the amount legally permitted to be collected for the leased premises, the landlord may upon determination that the rent is excessive, terminate this lease upon ten days' written notice and at the expiration of such ten days the lease shall terminate as though that were the date originally fixed herein for its termination and thereupon the tenant shall be a statutory tenant for such term and upon such conditions as the statute may provide." 

—19 

289

The second proposed clause reads as follows: 

"If, by reason of any legislative enactment of the Federal, State or City Government or governmental regulation of any agency of the Federal, State or City Government, or otherwise, it shall become unlawful or impossible for the landlord to collect the full amount of the rent herein provided, the landlord shall have the right to cancel this lease on 10 days' notice in writing to the tenant.”

The inclusion of either of the above clauses or any similar clauses would be in violation of the express provisions of 

Section 33 (2) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. 

Section 15 of the Regulations specifically provides that "An agreement by the tenant to waive the benefit of any provision of the Act or these Regulations is void." A tenant may not, therefore, agree to waive any provision of the Regulations except that he may agree to pay an increase in the maximum rent not to exceed 15 percent, provided he secures a lease for a period of at least two years upon the conditions specified in Section 33(2).

§ 33 (2)—Rooming Houses-Effect of Lease Increase for One Rate Upon Other Rates. Administrator's Opinion No. 77. 

A room in a rooming house is registered at different rates for one-, two-, and three-person occupancy. The present tenant who occupies at the two-person rate has executed a lease with the landlord, complying with all requirements of Section 33(2) of the Regulations and providing for a 15 percent increase in the maximum rent. The landlord now requests advice as to whether the execution of the lease operates to increase the two other rates by 15 percent so that in the event the tenant vacates, the landlord upon renting at a three-person rate may automatically collect 15 percent above that rate. 

The lease operates to increase the maximum rent only for the two-person rate in effect immediately prior to the execution of the lease. It does not operate to increase the one-person or three-person rates or the rates that may be in effect for varying terms of occupancy. If the tenant vacates and the room is subsequently rented for a different term of occupancy or number of occupants, that particular rate may be increased by lease only where the lease conforms with all the requirements of Section 33(2) of the Regulations. 291

§ 33(2)-Removal of Television Antenna by Landlord Where Installed Prior to March 1, 1950. See Administrator's Opinion No. 75 Under Section 24. 

§ 33(2)—Effect of Execution of Subsequent Lease Prior to the Expiration of the First Two Years of Prior Lease. Administrator's Opin ion No. 93. 

On June 1, 1951 a tenant entered into a voluntary 15% lease which complied with all of the requirements of Section 33(2) of the Regulations. The rent provided for in the lease was $46.00 per month and there has been no change in the maximum rent since the effective date of said lease. The tenant vacated the apartment on April 1, 1953 and it is now vacant. The landlord now desires to rent the housing accommodations to a new tenant and proposes to enter into a new voluntary lease for a term commencing April 15, 1953 and expiring May 30, 1955 at a monthly rental of $46.00 until June 1, 1953 and of $52.90, which is a further increase of 15%, from June 1, 1953 to the expiration of the lease. He requests advice as to whether the new voluntary lease will be effective to increase the maximum rent to $52.90 on June 1, 1953.

Yes. The execution of a lease as indicated will have the effect of increasing the maximum rent pursuant to Section. 

33(2) of the Regulations to $52.90 on June 1, 1953. 

§ 33 (2)—Lease Executed by Tenant Under Threat of Eviction Is Ineffectual to Increase Maximum Rents. Administrator's Opinion No. 109. 

A landlord of an apartment house in which a resident superintendent is required by law files an application for an Eviction Certificate pursuant to Section 54 of the Reg ulations, in which he claims he needs the apartment for a superintendent. Previously, the landlord had demanded that this particular tenant execute a 15% lease increase and the tenant had refused. After filing the application, the landlord advised the tenant that he would withdraw 292

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

it if the tenant would now give him an increase by lease. 

Rather than be evicted, the tenant complied. The landlord thereafter filed an application against another tenant under Section 54. The first tenant now asks whether he must pay this additional 15% called for in the lease. 

Section 33(2) of the Regulations specifies that a lease agreement must be voluntary and in good faith. On these facts, it is clear that the tenant executed the lease under duress since there was an actual threat of eviction. The act of the tenant cannot be considered voluntary and the lease does not comply with the statutory requirements. 

It is, therefore, the opinion of the Administrator that the lease was ineffectual to increase the maximum rent for the apartment. The Commission may institute an administrative proceeding pursuant to Section 36 of the Regulations to determine the facts. Upon this statement of facts, the Commission would issue an order re-establishing the maximum rent at the original sum prior to the execution of the lease. 

§ 33 (2)--Effect of the Execution of a Lease During the Pendency of Pro ceedings to Increase Rents Pursuant to Section 33(5); Meaning of

Term "increase over the maximum rent then in effect." Administra tor's Opinion No. 119. 

On July 1, 1957 a landlord of an apartment house filed an application for rent increases pursuant to Section 33(5) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. The tenant of an apartment has informed the landlord that he will vacate his apartment on November 30, 1957. The present maximum rent is $135.00 per month and the landlord anticipates an increase in rent to $155.25, effective September 1, 1957, pursuant to Section 33 (5) of the Regulations. No order has as yet been issued and the landlord desires to re-rent the apartment to a new tenant without waiting for the orders granting the anticipated rent increase. Any orders 293

increasing the maximum rents will be made effective as of September 1, 1957. The landlord seeks advice as to whether he can obtain a statutory lease increase of 15% from the new tenant based upon the anticipated increase in the maximum rent so as to be able to obtain a double 

15% increase. He submits two alternatives, as follows: "1. A lease for $135.00 plus the percentage of increase estimated by the landlord plus 15% with a savings clause prescribing the fact that the parties agree there is now an application under Section 33(5) which will adjust the maximum rent if granted in an amount retroactive to September 1, 1957; that it is the intention that the rent to be paid by the tenant to the landlord shall at all times be no more than 15% above the award to be made in the pending proceeding. "2. The alternative is to write a lease at $135.00 plus 

15% with an escalator clause limited to the precise amount which will be granted in the new pending Section 33 (5) proceeding issued retroactive to a date prior to execution and negotiation of the lease." The inclusion of either of the above alternatives would be in violation of the expressed provisions of Section 33 (2) 

of the Rent and Eviction Regulations. Section 33(2) spe cifically provides that the maximum rent shall be automatically increased by the execution of "said lease, provided such lease (b) Provides for a rent increase not in excess of 15 percent over the maximum rent then in effect; (e) Does not provide for the payment by the tenant of any rent in excess of the amount therein provided, unless the maximum rent is thereafter increased by order of the Administrator to which the tenant has consented pursuant to paragraph 1 of this section * * * The first alternative violates subparagraph (b) of Section 33(2). The 15% lease increase may not be superim- 294

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

posed on the anticipated higher rental which was not yet in effect since the lease increase in rent must be predicated upon the maximum rent which was actually in existence when its was executed. Although the Local Rent Administrator may thereafter order an increase in the rent retroactive to a date prior to the date when the lease was executed it cannot be said that the new rent was in existence when the lease was executed. The effect of a retroactive increase in rent is solely to permit the collection of the higher rent from the date prescribed as the effective date. of the rent increase. Factually the new rent did not come into existence until the date of the order fixing it. In a similar situation the Administrator took the same position and was upheld by the Court (Diemac Holding Co. v Weaver, Supreme Court, New York County, Tilzer, J., NYLJ, June 11, 1957, p 6, cols 7 and 8; affirmed without opinion, App Div, 1st Dept NYLJ, October 10, 1957, p 6, col 3). 

The second alternative proposal violates subparagraph (e) of Section 32(2) quoted above which permits such a further increase in a lease rent only where it is based on an order for increases in service, equipment, etc. to which the tenant has consented under Section 33(1). Obviously the tenant has not consented to the increase in this case. 

§ 33(3)-Adjustment for Additional Occupants or Subletting. Administrator's Opinion No. 25 [Revoked]. 

§ 33(5)—Valuation to be Used for the Computation of 4 Percent Return. 

Administrator's Opinion No. 85 [Revoked]. 

§ 33(5)—Application for Increase Not Proper Where Additional Services or Equipment Were Supplied at Time Order Adjusting Maxi mum Rent Was Granted Pursuant to Section 33(5). Administrator's

Opinion No. 108. 

The landlord of an apartment house installed new and superior modern gas ranges and new and superior mechanical refrigerators in several apartments, with the consent of the tenants in occupancy. Although the landlord would, 295

presumably, have been entitled to increases in the maximum rents of the apartments pursuant to Section 33(1) because of these improvements, he did not file any applications for rent increases based upon these installations. Instead, the landlord filed an application for adjustments of the maximum rents of all the apartments in the building pursuant to Section 33(5), and orders were issued granting him increases to bring his net income to the 6% required by the Statute and Regulations. Shortly after receiving these increases under Section 33(5), the landlord filed new applications under the provisions of Section 33(1) for further increases of the newly established maximum rents only as to those units where he had previously installed the new and better equipment. Will orders be issued further increasing the rents of these apartments pursuant to Section 33(1)?

No. The new maximum rents established pursuant to 

Section 33(5) includes all essential services, furniture, furnishings and equipment provided, or required to be provided, on the date of the issuance of the orders. It therefore follows that a landlord cannot obtain rent increases based upon a claim of additional services or equipment which were being provided on this date. 

The landlord could have elected to first file his application pursuant to Section 33(1) of the Regulations and, in a proper case, receive rental increases for those apartments which had received the new and superior equipment. Had he done so, he could have subsequently filed applications pursuant to Section 33(5), but the additional rental income would have been reflected in his later applications. 

To permit the landlord to obtain additional rent increases upon grounds for adjustment already in existence prior to the filing of the application pursuant to Section 33(5) would be to permit circumvention and misuse of the Section. 296

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS § 33(5)-Effect of the Execution of a Lease During the Pendency of Pro ceedings to Increase Rents Pursuant to Section 33(5). See Admin istrator's Opinion No. 119 Under Section 33(2). 

Section 34. Grounds for decrease of maximum rent. 

§ 34(2) Decrease in Service Prior to March 1, 1950. Administrator's Opinion No. 27. 

In February, 1950 a landlord installed a separate electric meter for his tenant, even though prior thereto the landlord was obligated to, and did furnish electricity. On 

March 1, 1950, the tenant paid for his own electricity. May the tenant then file an application for a rent reduction based upon a decrease in service? 

Yes. Section 34(2) of the Regulations gives him the right to file an application for a rent reduction since there has been a decrease of a service required to be provided, even if not actually provided, on March 1, 1950. 

§ 34(2)—Decrease in Service of Painting and Decorating Upon Renting to New Tenant. Administrator's Opinion No. 105 (For New York 

City only). 

On November 1, 1954 a tenant rented a vacant apartment and upon the landlord's refusal to paint and decorate the apartment, the tenant painted it at his own expense. After taking possession the tenant filed an application with the 

Local Rent Administrator for a reduction of the maximum rent for the apartment pursuant to Section 34(2) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations based upon a claimed decrease in the service of painting and decorating. What action will be taken by the Administrator upon this application of the tenant? 

Should the tenant apply within one year from the time of rental he is entitled to relief. A copy of the application will be forwarded to the landlord and he will be required to produce his books and records showing the painting practice of the apartment. If this apartment was due for 297

a painting at the time the tenant took possession, the landlord's failure to paint and decorate would be deemed a reduction in the services required to be provided by the landlord. Within the City of New York there is a presumption that apartments in multiple dwelling houses were customarily painted, prior to March 1, 1943, at periodic two-year intervals. The Appellate Courts have sustained this position of the Administrator in Kathy Realty Corp. v. McGoldrick (281 App Div 850, 119 NYS2d 255) and 

Bersam Holding Corp. v. McGoldrick (128 NYS2d 557).

This presumption is subject to rebuttal by the production of the landlord of books and records establishing a longer periodic period prior to that date. In any event, the burden is upon the landlord to establish that the apartment was not as yet due for a painting as of the time the new tenant took possession. Should the landlord fail to establish this, it would follow that there had been a reduction in the service of painting and decorating. The landlord would be given an opportunity to promptly refund the actual cost to the tenant of painting and decorating the apartment providing the cost is reasonable. Should the landlord fail to do this, the Local Rent Administrator would issue an order reducing the maximum rent because of the failure of the landlord to maintain the essential service of painting and decorating. The landlord cannot relieve himself of this obligation to paint and decorate by inserting a clause in the lease whereby the tenant agrees to do the painting. 

§ 34(3) Decrease of Maximum Rent for Illegal Rooming House Where 

Maximum Rents for Single-Room Occupancy Had Previously Been 

Registered or Established by Order. Administrator's Opinion No. 

112 (For New York City only).

In 1948 the owner of a one-family house rented the individual rooms in the house for single-room occupancy. 298

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Ac- Under the Federal Rent Regulations this structure was considered to be a rooming house when so rented. cordingly, the owner registered the rents for the individual rooms with the Federal authorities, thereby establishing maximum rents for the rooms. Recently the Department of Housing and Buildings of the City of New York certified to the Commission that the single-room occupancy is a violation of local law. What action will be taken by the Commission?

The Administrator will institute action pursuant to Section 34(3) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations to decrease the maximum rents. That section authorizes the Administrator to order a decrease of the maximum rent on the ground that the housing accommodations are occupied in violation of law. It was not the intent of the Legislature to permit landlords to reap profits from the continued illegal operation of a building. Therefore, the maximum rents will be reduced to a level which would reflect the seriousness of the condition which is certified by the municipal department.

Section 34(3) provides that the Administrator may order a reduction in the maximum rent where "a municipal department having jurisdiction certifies that the housing accommodation is a fire hazard or is in continued dangerous condition or detrimental to life or health, or is occupied in violation of law." The same approach will be taken where any illegal condition is certified to the Administrator by the appropriate municipal department. 299

Section 35. Decrease of services; application, order or report. 

§ 35(1)-Change from Manually-Operated Elevators to Automatic Elevators. Administrator's Opinion No. 40. 

A landlord requests advice from a Local Rent Office as to whether he may convert from manually-operated elevators to automatic elevators. What shall we advise him?

No conversion from manually-operated elevators to automatic elevators may be made except by an order of the 

Local Rent Administrator. Section 24 of the Regulations requires that a landlord shall continue to provide with the housing accommodations the same essential services as were provided on the date determining the maximum rent. 

A change from a manually-operated elevator to an automatic elevator, depending upon the facts in the case, may constitute a decrease in service. It must therefore be undertaken only by an order pursuant to the requirements of Section 35(1) of the Regulations. The landlord should file an application requesting that an order be issued by the 

Local Rent Administrator permitting the change. The landlord may use a master application, but must furnish to the Local Rent Office sufficient copies so that one may be sent to each tenant. After the record has been thoroughly examined, the Local Rent Administrator may, in his discretion, either grant the application or deny it. 

The policy determination in the conversion from manually-operated to automatic elevators is whether it constitutes a decrease in services. The general character of the house, the number of elevators, the number of entrances and lobbies, and the other services performed by the elevator personnel are factors to be considered. There may not be a case of a decrease in services where upon conversion the overall service and the type of automatic elevators to be 300

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

installed give the tenants substantially as good service as they previously enjoyed. However, the elimination of some or all of the elevator employees may result in a loss to the tenants of certain protection furnished by their presence and of various other services performed by them. 

An order permitting conversion would usually require that the building entrance or entrances and the lobby not be left unattended for any portion of the day or night. 

Such order may also require that the landlord install facilities such as inter-communicating system; vestibule, bell, and buzzer system; and U. S. Government-approved mail boxes; or other provisions for furnishing services previously performed by elevator employees. The establishment of these conditions in the order and the landlord's compliance therewith may constitute no decrease in services because of the conversion. 

Should an order be granted by the Local Rent Administrator, it shall clearly set forth the terms and conditions upon which the permission to convert is granted. In addition, the order should contain a statement that “This order does not affect any rights the tenant may have under any rental or other contractual agreement with the landlord." 

§ 35(1)—Change from Submetering Electrical Current to a Rent Inclusion Plan. Administrator's Opinion No. 80. 

A landlord requests advice from a Local Rent Office as to the procedure which he may follow due to the fact that a recent order of the Public Service Commission has the effect of prohibiting the submetering of electric current. 

He states that on the date determining the maximum rent he submetered the electrical current and has continued to charge for such service on the basis of the tenants' actual consumption; and that he desires to effectuate a change- 301

over in the furnishing of electric service and adopt a rent inclusion plan with the tenants. What shall we advise him? 

Under the Regulations, the housing accommodation has a varying rent since the varying charge for electrical current each month is added to the base rent of the apartment which thereby constitutes the maximum rent. The amount of the charge, therefore, whether measured by meter or otherwise, is considered a part of the rent paid by the tenant for the use and occupancy of the premises and for the services furnished in connection therewith. 

The landlord may therefore proceed in the following manner. A landlord who wishes to discontinue his present submetering practice and desires to give unlimited electrical current to tenants under a rent inclusion plan may file an application pursuant to Section 35(1) requesting permission of the Local Rent Administrator to discontinue furnishing metered electrical current, and for permission to remove the meters in the individual apartments. One master application may be filed with sufficient additional copies to be served on the tenants. The application should state that the landlord will furnish unmetered current in return for the establishment of the maximum rent at a fixed amount, instead of the varying amount now in effect. The landlord must submit evidence as to the actual experienced 12-month electric bill for each apartment. The landlord must also submit evidence of the actual experienced 12month electric bill for each type or class of apartment. 

It is suggested that the application be prepared so as to contain the following items: 1. type of apartment (1, 2 or 3 

rooms) and the designation of each apartment affected; 2. the base legal maximum rent exclusive of any electric charge; 3. average of the actual experienced 12-month electric bill for that apartment; 4. the average of the actual experienced 12-month electric bill for that type or class 302

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

of apartment; 5. the total rent requested which will include the base rent and the amount for unlimited electric service. 

The Local Rent Administrator may then issue an order granting permission to the landlord to change from furnishing electrical current on a metered basis to an unmetered basis, granting permission to remove the meters and establishing the maximum rent at a fixed and definite amount each month. This new maximum rent would include the previous base rent for each apartment plus the amount designated in item 4 above.

However, having given due regard to the equities involved affecting tenants in possession on May 1, 1952, the State Rent Administrator has determined that such order will also establish an alternate maximum rent which will include the previous base rent for such apartment plus the average of the actual experienced 12-month bills for that apartment, and such alternate maximum rent will be in effect so long as that tenant remains in possession. 

If the landlord requests an increase in excess of the fixed maximum rent to be determined in the manner described in the foregoing paragraph upon the assumption that the furnishing of unmetered electric current to tenants may result in substantial increases in electrical consumption, he must obtain the written consents of tenants to such an increase and attach them to his application. The reasonableness of the increase requested in excess of the amount as established by the procedure in the preceding paragraph will be determined by the Local Rent Administrator on the basis of the information, facts and figures submitted by the landlord in his application. If the landlord succeeds in securing the written consent of tenants in occupancy of at least 75% of the housing accommodations in the structure, the consent of the minority of the tenants would not be required and the Local Rent Administrator could issue 303

orders granting the increases as stated above affecting all of the housing accommodations in the structure. If the landlord does not secure the written consents of at least 75% of the tenants in the structure, then orders granting such increases would be issued only against those tenants who have consented. Tenants who have not consented would have their rent fixed merely on the basis of the procedure outlined in the preceding paragraph. 

No landlord can be required to adopt a rent inclusion plan. A landlord who desires to adopt a rent inclusion plan must make it available to all his tenants. A tenant who does not desire to participate in a rent inclusion plan may make his own arrangement directly with the utility company. However, the landlord must give the utility company serving the area any written authorization which may be required and must also permit the tenant to use the present electrical wiring and to install any additional wiring which may be required at the tenant's own expense.

§ 36-Order Issued by O.H.E. Between March 1, 1949 and March 1, 1950 Based Upon Additional Services and Facilities. Administrator's Opin ion No. 21 (For New York City only).

A housing accommodation within the City of New York had a maximum rent of $50.00 per month on March 1, 1949.

On January 10, 1950, an order was issued by the Office of the Housing Expediter increasing the maximum rent to $60.00 per month. This order was based upon both an increase in service and equipment and an increase in operating costs. What is the maximum rent?

The Local Rent Administrator will determine the maximum rent pursuant to Section 36 of the Regulations. This order will be issued on the basis of the maximum rent in effect on March 1, 1949 plus the increase in rental value because of the increase in services or facilities to which the tenant had agreed.

March 1, 1950, the maximum rent is in doubt. The Local

Rent Administrator will therefore determine the maximum rent pursuant to Section 36 of the Regulations. This order will be issued on the basis of the maximum rent in effect on March 1, 1949, minus the decrease in rental value because of the decrease in the service of painting and decorating.

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS PART V-EVICTIONS Part V-Eviction by Municipality Because of Violation of Local Ordinances. Administrator's Opinion No. 38. 

Certain housing accommodations occupied by a tenant are in violation of local ordinances because of their unsanitary condition and the fact that they are not fit for human habitation as housing accommodations. Pursuant to such local ordinances, the municipality desires to take legal steps to have the tenant removed because of such conditions. Is the eviction of the tenant by the municipality subject to the Rent and Eviction Regulations?

Since the person seeking the eviction of the tenant is not the landlord of the housing accommodation but is a municipality proceeding under local ordinances, the Temporary State Housing Rent Commission will not interfere or intervene in the proceeding to remove the tenant. 

Section 51. Restrictions on removal of tenant including hotel tenants. $ 51(2)-Self-Created Necessity-Additional Occupants. See Adminis trator's Opinion No. 69 Under Section 55(1). 

§ 51(2)—Attempt to Evict Tenant Who Occupies Residential Housing Accommodations Under Commercial Lease Executed As a Device to Evade the Rent Regulations. Administrator's Opinion No. 102. 

An apartment has a registered maximum rent of $61.00 per month. When it becomes vacant the landlord rents it by commercial lease in the sum of $85.00 per month, the lease reciting that the tenant is to practice his profession as a podiatrist in the apartment. Actually the tenant practices his profession as a podiatrist in another building and uses the accommodations from the very inception of his tenancy exclusively for residential purposes. The tenant becomes aware of the registered maximum rent of $61.00 307

per month and complains either to the landlord, or to the Local Rent Office which then notifies the landlord. 

The landlord thereafter attempts to evict the tenant pursuant to Section 52(1) of the Regulations upon the ground that the tenant is violating a substantial obligation of his tenancy by not using the apartment for the practice of his profession. The tenant asserts that the landlord knew from the inception of his lease that he would use it only for housing purposes and that this attempt to evict him is retaliatory because he has asserted his rights under the 

Rent Regulations. May the tenant be evicted?

The device of using a commercial lease agreement in order to secure more than the maximum rent for the apartment will not deprive the tenant of his rights under the Regulations. Therefore, if a court finds the facts to be as stated by the tenant he may not be evicted since Section 

10(2) of the Emergency Housing Rent Control Law, as amended, and Section 51(2) of the Regulations, specifically prohibit the eviction of any tenant where the attempt to evict the tenant is made ". because such tenant or occupant has taken, or proposes to take action authorized or required by the Act or any regulations, order or requirement thereunder." The tenant was within his legal rights in refusing to pay more than the maximum legal rent of the accommodations since, on the facts, this commercial lease was an attempt to evade the residential rent ceilings. 

Consequently he may not now be evicted upon the ground that he is violating a substantial obligation of his tenancy. 

Pursuant to Section 14 of the Regulations, the Court can find that the provision of the lease under which the landlord is attempting to evict the tenant cannot be enforced as it is inconsistent with the Act and these Regulations. 

The signing of the commercial lease by the tenant cannot be deemed a waiver of his rights since Section 16 of the 308

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

Regulations specifically provides that an agreement by the tenant to waive the benefit of any provision of the Act or the Regulations is void. 

Section 52. Proceedings for eviction without certificate.. 

§ 52-Preliminary Notice Required in Cases of Violation of Substantial Obligation of Tenancy. Administrator's Opinion No. 64. 

A landlord served a notice upon his tenant to vacate a housing accommodation alleging that the tenant has violated a substantial obligation of his tenancy as set forth in Section 52(1) of the Regulations. Within forty-eight hours, an exact copy of this notice, together with an affidavit of service, was filed with the Local Rent Office in accordance with the requirements of Section 53. The tenant complained to the Local Rent Office that he had not received any previous notice from the landlord that he was violating a substantial obligation of his tenancy and that he was given no previous opportunity to cease the violation. Has the landlord complied with all the requirements of Sections 

52 and 53 of the Regulations?

No. The landlord has not complied with the requirements of Section 52 and, therefore, may not now commence a court action to remove or evict the tenant. Sections 52(1) and 53 provide for two successive notices. The first must be a preliminary notice under Section 52(1) to inform the tenant of the violation and to afford him an opportunity of curing the same. If the tenant has not cured the violation within the time period set forth in this first notice which must be at least 10 days, the landlord may then serve the notice to vacate required under Section 53. The only exception to this requirement of a preliminary notice in such a case is where "within the three-month period immediately prior to the commencement of the proceeding, the tenant 309

has wilfully violated such an obligation, inflicting serious and substantial injury to the landlord." § 52-Tenant Fails to Vacate After Notifying Landlord of His Intention to Do So. See Administrator's Opinion No. 71 Under Section 54. 

§ 52(3)-Eviction of Tenants because Building Violations Are Placed Against the Premises. Administrator's Opinion No. 114. 

The Administrator has received numerous requests for advice from landlords as to whether in the following situations they may evict tenants by proceeding directly in the Municipal Court under Section 52 (3) of the Rent and Eviction Regulations: (1) Where the Department of Buildings has placed violations against the building because it does not contain an adequate number of water closets; (2) Where the Department of Buildings has placed violations against the building because needed repairs, painting and maintenance have not been provided; (3) Where the Department of Buildings has placed violations against the building because of a failure to secure approval of alterations or to file necessary plans. 

In some instances, landlords claimed that the financial expenditure necessary to remove the violations is not warranted and, therefore, instead of spending money to cure the illegal condition, they wish to evict the tenants in order to board up or demolish the building. In other cases, landlord claimed that in order to remove the violations, it is necessary that the tenants surrender possession of their apartments. However, the landlords intend to rerent the apartments after the alterations are completed. 

In the opinion of the Administrator, a landlord may not proceed directly in the Municipal Court under Section 52(3) to evict tenants in any of the above three situations. Section 52(3) of the Regulations, derived from Section 5(c) of the Act, permits the eviction of tenants where "occupancy 310

ADMINISTRATOR'S INTERPRETATIONS 

of the housing accommodation by the tenant is illegal because of the requirements of law, and the landlord is subject to civil or criminal penalties therefor or both.” 

The existence of Department of Buildings violations does not necessarily make the tenant's occupancy illegal. Both the statutory and administrative intent was to authorize the eviction of a tenant to cure a building violation only where the tenant's occupancy is itself the violation of law (e.g., where a third tenant occupies an illegal basement or attic space in a two-family house and the accommodation cannot be legalized by a reasonable expenditure of money). In that circumstance, it is the tenant's occupancy of the space as a housing accommodation which is illegal and causes the violation. In the three situations enumerated above, the violations did not result from, nor are they related to the tenant's occupancy of the apartment. These violations have resulted solely from the landlord's failure to complete installations, alterations or repairs to the building as required by law. Therefore, Section 52(3) would not be applicable.

A landlord may proceed under Section 52(3) where the tenant's occupancy of the housing accommodation is illegal (e.g., where a third tenant occupies an illegal basement or attic space in a two-family house and the accommodation cannot be legalized by a reasonable expenditure of money), but only if the landlord intends to discontinue permanently the housing use of the space. Under no circumstance may a landlord proceed under that section where he seeks to discontinue temporarily the use of the housing accommodation in order to remove violations and thereafter rerent. 

Even if the required alterations cannot be safely completed with the tenants remaining in possession, the landlord may not evict them but may make application to the Local 

Rent Administrator for their temporary removal. Where 311

warranted, relief will be granted under Section 54 and appropriate conditions imposed. Where a landlord seeks to withdraw permanently an entire building from the rental market, he may obtain certificates of eviction under Section 59(2) upon establishing his compliance with the conditions therein provided.

Section 53. Notices required in proceedings under Section 

52. 

§ 53—Notice as Affecting Requirements of Real Property Law. Administrator's Opinion No. 49. 

A landlord wishes to evict his tenant upon the ground that the tenant is using the housing accommodation for an immoral or illegal purpose. The tenant is a month to month tenant. The landlord wishes to know whether he must give the tenant a thirty-day notice required by Section 232 of the Real Property Law in addition to the ten-day notice prescribed by Section 53. 

Section 53 of the Regulations does not change Section 232 of the Real Property Law. Section 53 merely sets forth minimum notice requirements under the Regulations and does not affect the tenant's term of occupancy under his rental agreement. The service of the thirty-day notice is required by State law in order to terminate a month to month or monthly tenancy. The obligation to give this notice and the manner of serving said notice are not affected by Section 53. However, the landlord may incorporate the requirements of Section 53 in his thirty-day notice. He is under no obligation to serve separate notices. 

§ 53-Preliminary Notice Required in Cases of Violation of Substantial Obligation of Tenancy. See Administrator's Opinion No. 64 Under Section 52. 

§ 53-Landlord Claims Occupant to Be a Squatter. See Administrator's

Opinion No. 57 Under Section 56.
